Now the marking rules per case:
1. CASE DISPUTED: the situation itself, in ordinary terms before theory, decides a point against the fixed verdict.
2. DISAGREE: on at least one point, the theory's verdict reaches a different finding from the fixed verdict.
3. SPLIT: on at least one point, the theory's sentences support two readings that reach different findings, and the text leaves the choice open.
4. SILENT: on at least one point, the theory leaves the finding open (input the case leaves unstated, or sentences stop short).
5. AGREE: on every point, same finding.

**O14 - The phrasebook.** Verdict: "Kofi has kept a narrow skill by his own effort. He has yet to understand the language."
Points: (1) Kofi has kept a narrow skill by his own effort. (2) He has yet to understand the language.

**O15 - Sam's inherited marks.** Verdict: "Sam's account of his own work is complete. Where the arrows came from is a hole in the history of the diagrams, and it is a hole in his account, because his cut followed them."
Points: (1) Sam's account of his own work is complete. (2) Where the arrows came from is a hole in the history of the diagrams. (3) It is a hole in his account, because his cut followed them.

**O16 - The worn key and the diary.** Verdict: "The worn key is evidence on its own. The diary is the diagram talking about itself. Sam has one independent witness, and the diary is a second copy of the first."
Points: (1) The worn key is evidence on its own. (2) The diary is the diagram talking about itself. (3) Sam has one independent witness. (4) The diary is a second copy of the first.

**O17 - The robot's log and the maker's manual.** Verdict: "Ownership here rests on the log, which shows the routine running inside the robot. Strike the manual's sentence and nothing is lost; keep only the manual's sentence and there is nothing."
Points: (1) Ownership here rests on the log. (2) The log shows the routine running inside the robot. (3) Strike the manual's sentence and nothing is lost. (4) Keep only the manual's sentence and there is nothing.

**O18 - The one tooth.** Verdict: "The one tooth is hers. The rest is relay. She built something, small, and it is exactly the tooth."
Points: (1) The one tooth is hers. (2) The rest is relay. (3) She built something, small, and it is exactly the tooth.

**O19 - The diagram in his hand.** Verdict: "The diagram was in his hand and did no work; the lock did it. Had the diagram said something different, he would have followed the lock."
Points: (1) The diagram was in his hand and did no work. (2) The lock did it. (3) Had the diagram said something different, he would have followed the lock.

**O20 - Two valves in one second.** Verdict: "Both did something that would have stopped it. Neither can claim it alone. The record shows two sufficient acts and no way to say whose water stopped."
Points: (1) Both did something that would have stopped it. (2) Neither can claim it alone. (3) The record shows two sufficient acts and no way to say whose water stopped.

**O21 - The expert's question.** Verdict: "The robot chose, with its own evidence. The expert did the part that took understanding, knowing what would separate the two. The choosing is the robot's; the diagnosis as an achievement is mostly the expert's."
Points: (1) The robot chose, with its own evidence. (2) The expert did the part that took understanding, knowing what would separate the two. (3) The choosing is the robot's. (4) The diagnosis as an achievement is mostly the expert's.

**O22 - Two knobs, one linkage.** Verdict: "Her question is about the pair. The brief lead is part of what 'together' means on this machine, and it is a fact about the machine, not a second question about one knob."
Points: (1) Her question is about the pair. (2) The brief lead is part of what "together" means on this machine. (3) It is a fact about the machine, not a second question about one knob.

**O23 - "The seal was tight".** Verdict: "She added a dimension. Mentioning tightness in passing did not make it something that could vary in her account; measuring it did."
Points: (1) She added a dimension. (2) Mentioning tightness in passing did not make it something that could vary in her account. (3) Measuring it did.

**O24 - The unused joint setting.** Verdict: "They are two arrangements, and the record has not chosen between them. One reachable setting is enough."
Points: (1) They are two arrangements. (2) The record has not chosen between them. (3) One reachable setting is enough.

**O25 - One second apart.** Verdict: "Ana stopped it. Ben's act would have stopped it and did not, because there was nothing left to stop when it came."
Points: (1) Ana stopped it. (2) Ben's act would have stopped it. (3) Ben's act did not stop it, because there was nothing left to stop when it came.

**O26 - Two feeds, one moment.** Verdict: "Both did the work, and the record divides it: each stopped the half that ran through their feed."
Points: (1) Both did the work. (2) The record divides it. (3) Each stopped the half that ran through their feed.

But consider: for an ordinary human agent performing an act, the boundary is trivial (the person). The theory's declaration requirement is a modelling discipline. When the case describes a person doing something herself with no competing claimant and no composite structure, the boundary is not "the deciding matter" — the deciding matter is who performed the process (stated). When the case describes a composite system (robot + uploaded routine, robot + module) or an external contributor (technician whispering), the boundary IS the deciding matter.

Let me parse: sig_C(j) = {(a,b,L_j(a,b))}. A measurement's signature is "invariant under interventions on the measured port and variable under edits to the measuring relation". Hmm — an intervention on the measured port changes the measured port's value; the measurement's relation (how it maps the measured thing to the reading) stays the same → the signature (the relation) is invariant ✓. And "change only the reading and the part it reports stays as it was; change the part and the reading follows" — this is the informal gloss: if you change the reading (an observation edit on the measuring relation), the measured part is unchanged; if you change the measured part, the reading follows. Hmm.

The tester's complaint: "what stays invariant (the relation, not the reading) is left for the reader to supply" — i.e., the formal clause says the *signature* (relation) is invariant under interventions on the measured port, while the informal says the *reading* follows changes of the part — these talk about different things (relation vs reading) and the reader must work out that the invariant is the relation. Hmm — is this a real tension? "invariant under interventions on the measured port" — hmm, hold on: if you intervene on the measured port, does the measurement's signature stay the same? Hmm — for a measurement, intervening on the measured port shouldn't change the *measuring relation* (the calibration), just the value flowing through. So the signature (relation) is invariant ✓ while the reading (a value) changes. And the informal gloss says "change the part and the reading follows" ✓ consistent (the reading changes, the relation doesn't). So both are consistent once you see that invariance attaches to the relation and the reading is a value. The tester says this is "left for the reader to supply" — a presentational obscurity. Hmm — CONFIRMED or DISPUTED? The item claims an uneasiness/tension. Hmm. 10. Part IX: "A route that started and did no work, or that was already at rest when the result occurred, is not active for that result; whether a route is active is read from the history, not from the result." ✓

11. Part XI: "\(\operatorname{ProducedBy}\) holds when an active route (Part IX) runs from \(\Delta\) to the repair; it credits each contribution the history establishes, and where two sufficient contributions both ran, both are credited and the history supplies no division of credit that it does not contain." ✓
